\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}},p)

(level 1/2)

for every
p\in\{q_{0},q_{1}\}
and
u\in\{a,b\}
.

Reversing derivations on each level

We will show an analogue of

(2)
at levels
i>0
, by induction on
i
. Since
a step on level
i>0
involves an entire derivation on level
i-1
(which can
result in several nonterminals), the induction works with a stronger property
where the reverse derivation can not only start in one produced nonterminal (as
in
(2)
), but arbitrary vectors in
\mathbb{N}^{R_{i}}
.
But then, suppose we want to reverse

(p,s^{x},q)\,\Rightarrow_{0}\,(p,s^{x},t)+(t,s^{x},q).

Deriving
(p,s^{x},q)
from
(p,s^{x},t)+(t,s^{x},q)
would require either

(p,s^{x},t)
or
(t,s^{x},q)
to derive a vector without nonterminals, which would
be too strong a condition. However, we can use a slight relaxation: We will
show that derivations can be reversed
up to a byproduct of cycles

b+{b}^{\dagger}
. For instance, in our example, the condition
(2)
yields

(p,s^{x},t)\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{0}\,(p,s^{x},q)+{(t,s^{x},q)}^{\dagger}
and hence

(p,s^{x},t)+(t,s^{x},q)\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{0}\,(p,s^{x},q)+{(t,s^{x},q)}^{\dagger}+(t,s^{x},q).

To
make “up to cycles
b+{b}^{\dagger}
” precise, we now introduce the
equivalence relations
\approx_{a}
.

First, we define the relation
\leadsto
on
N
as follows. For
a\in N_{i}
and

b\in N_{[i,k]}
, we have
a\leadsto b
iff there is a configuration

\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{N}^{N_{[i,k]}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T_{[i,k]}}
with
\mathbf{u}(b)\geq 1
and
a\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}
. In other words, for
a\in N_{i}
and
b\in N_{[i,k]}
, we have
a\leadsto b

if
b
can be reached from
a
in one derivation step on level
i
.

Let
\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}
denote the reflexive transitive closure of
\leadsto
.
For each
a\in N_{i}
, we define the monoid
\Delta_{a}
, which is generated by all
b+{b}^{\dagger}
with
b\in N_{[i+1,k]}
and
a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b
. Hence,
\Delta_{a}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{N_{[i+1,k]}}
.
For
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{N_{[i,k]}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T_{[i,k]}}
,
we have
\mathbf{u}\approx_{a}\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
if and only if there are
\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\Delta_{a}
with
\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{d}=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}+\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
.
A
k
-grammar is called
i
-bidirected
if for
every
a\in R_{i}
and every derivation
a\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}
with

\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{N}^{R_{i}}
,
\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{0}
,
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{N}^{R_{[i,k]}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T_{[i,k]}}
, then

\mathbf{u}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,a+\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
for some
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
with
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\approx_{a}{\mathbf{v}}^{\dagger}
.
In short, up to differences in
\Delta_{a}
, we can
reverse derivations on level
i
that start in nonterminals in
R
.
Note that if
G
is
i
-bidirected, then on
R_{i}
, the relation
\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}

is symmetric and thus an equivalence.
We show the following:

Lemma 7.3.

If
G
is bidirected, then it is
i
-bidirected for each
i\in[0,k]
.

Expressing emptiness using cosets

An important ingredient in our proof is to work with integer linear

equations
instead of inequalities. While solution sets of systems of
inequalities are semilinear sets in
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, solution sets of equation systems
are cosets of
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. Recall that a coset in
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
is a set of the form

\mathbf{u}+U
, where
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and
U\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{m}
is a subgroup. In this
section, we will translate emptiness in bidirected grammars into a problem
about cosets of
\mathbb{Z}^{N\cup T}
. In general, we employ three operations for
constructing cosets. First, we can take
\mathbf{u}+\langle S\rangle
for

\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N\cup T}
and any set
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{N\cup T}
. Recall that

\langle S\rangle
is the subgroup generated by
S
. Second, if

C_{1},C_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{N\cup T}
are cosets, then their point-wise sum
C_{1}+C_{2}

is a coset, and the set
C_{1}\cap C_{2}
is either a coset or empty.

A central role will be played by the group
H_{a}
, which we define for each
a\in R_{i}
:

(1)

H_{a}=\langle-b+\mathbf{u}\mid\text{$b\in R_{i}$ and $b\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}$ and $a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b$}\rangle

Hence,
H_{a}
is the group generated by all differences that are added when applying derivation steps
b\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}
for
a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b
. We observe that if
a\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,\mathbf{u}
, then
-a+\mathbf{u}\in H_{a}
. We will need a coset to express that in such a derivation, there are no level-
i
letters (resp. no level-
i
terminals) left. We will do this by intersecting with the cosets

S_{i}=\mathbb{Z}^{N_{[i+1,k]}\cup T_{[i+1,k]}},\hskip 5.69046ptS^{\prime}_{i}=\mathbb{Z}^{N_{[i+1,k]}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T_{[i,k]}}.

Using
H_{a}
and
S_{i}
, we can now define the coset that will essentially
characterize
L(a)
. For
a\in R_{i}
, we set

(2)

L_{a}=(a+H_{a})\cap S_{i}.

One of the main results of this section will be that for
a\in R_{i}
,
we can describe
L(a)
in terms of
L_{a}
. Following the theme that we can do things
only “up to differences in
\Delta_{a}
” on each level, we need a group version of
\Delta_{a}
.
For every
a\in N_{i}
, we have the subgroup
D_{a}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{N_{i+1}}
, which we
define next. If
a\in N_{i}
with
i\in[0,k-1]
, we set
D_{a}=\langle b+{b}^{\dagger}\mid b\in N_{i+1},~a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b\rangle
. For
a\in N_{k}
, we define

D_{a}=\{\mathbf{0}\}
. In other words,
D_{a}
is the group generated by
\Delta_{a}
, for every
a\in N
.
With this notation, one of the crucial ingredients in our proof is to show that
L_{a}=L(a)+D_{a}
, i.e.
L_{a}
describes
L(a)
up
to differences in
D_{a}
(see
Theorem

7.4
). By our observation about
H_{a}
, it is obvious that

L(a)+D_{a}\subseteq L_{a}
. The key step is that also
L_{a}\subseteq L(a)+D_{a}
.

While the cosets
L_{a}
are coset versions of the sets
L(a)
, we will also need
coset versions of a slight variant of
L(a)
. For each
a\in R_{i}
, by
M(a)
, we denote the vectors that
are derivable from
a
, but may still contain level-
i
terminals:

M(a)=\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{N}^{N_{[i+1,k]}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T_{[i,k]}}\mid a\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,\mathbf{u}\}.

Thus,
M(a)
differs from
L(a)
by collecting all derivable
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{N}^{N_{[i+1,k]}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T}
, where all level-
i
nonterminals have been eliminated, but not necessarily all level-
i
terminals, meaning
L(a)=M(a)\cap S_{i}
. Just as
L_{a}
is a coset analogue of
L(a)
, we have a coset analogue of
M(a)
:

M_{a}=(a+H_{a})\cap S^{\prime}_{i}.

The cosets
M_{a}
will be needed to express, using cosets, whether
L(a)
is empty for nonterminals
a\in N_{i}
that do not necessarily belong to
R_{i}
: Those are the nonterminals for which we do not know whether
L(a)
is empty. Such sets
L(a)
do not directly correspond to cosets. However, we will be able to use cosets to characterize when
L(a)
is empty. Here, we use the cosets
K_{a}
for
a\in N_{i}
,
i\in[0,k-1]
:

K_{a}=(L_{a}+\langle-b+M_{b}\mid b\in R_{i+1},~a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b\rangle)\cap S_{i+1}.

We shall see that the coset
K_{a}
corresponds to those vectors in
\mathbb{N}^{N_{[i+1,k]}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T_{[i+1,k]}}
that can be derived using
a
.
Using the cosets
K_{b}
for
b\in N_{[0,k-1]}
, we will be able to characterize
emptiness of
L(a)
for
a\in N_{[1,k]}
. In order to do the same for
a\in N_{0}
, we need a final type of cosets. For each production
a\to\mathbf{u}
with
\mathbf{u}=b_{1}+\cdots+b_{n}+\mathbf{v}
in our grammar with
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in N_{0}
and

\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{T}
, we define

K_{a\to\mathbf{u}}=(M_{b_{1}}+\cdots+M_{b_{n}}+\mathbf{v})\cap S_{0}.

The key ingredient in our proof is the following analogue of the
translation of
Verma et al. 2005
to Presburger arithmetic. Here,
we express the set
L(a)
of derivable vectors of a nonterminal
a
as a coset.

Theorem 7.4.

If
G=(N,T,P)
is bidirected, then
M(a)+D_{a}=M_{a}
for every
a\in R
.
In particular,
L(a)+D_{a}=L_{a}
.

Intuition for the proof of
Theorem

7.4

Before we sketch the proof, let us compare it with the construction of
Verma et al. 2005
. They show that if we
assign to each production
p
in a context-free grammar a number
\mathbf{x}(p)

saying how often
p
is applied, then there is a derivation consistent with

\mathbf{x}
if and only if:

(i)

each
nonterminal is produced as many times as it is consumed (except for the
start-symbol, which is consumed once more) and

(ii)

the participating
nonterminals (i.e. for which
\mathbf{x}(p)>0
) must be
connected
: The latter
means, for each nonterminal, it must be possible to reach its consumed
nonterminal by way of productions that occur in
\mathbf{x}
.

Essentially, we will argue that for bidirected grammars, one can drop (ii).
This is because, independently of a particular derivation, each set
N_{i}

decomposes into connected components with respect to
\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}
. Then, instead
of stipulating connectedness of the set of used nonterminals, we only need all
used nonterminals to be in the same
\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}
-component. To construct a
derivation inside one
\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}
component, we need to show that there exist
“connecting derivations” between each pair in
\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}
.
This is made precise in the notion of Kirchhoff graphs, which we define next.

Kirchhoff graphs

Let
a\in R_{i}
. A
Kirchhoff graph for
a
is a directed graph whose set
of vertices is
\{b\in R_{i}\mid a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b\}
, such that there exists an edge

(b,c)
for each
b,c\in R_{i}
with
a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b
and
a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}c
, and where
an edge
(b,c)
is weighted by an element
\mathbf{g}_{b,c}\in\mathbb{N}^{N_{[i+1,k]}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T_{[i+1,k]}}
such that the following holds:

(i)

b\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,c+\mathbf{g}_{b,c}
for every
b,c
,

(ii)

\mathbf{g}_{b,b}=\mathbf{0}
for every
b
, and

(iii)

for any vertices
b,c,d
,
we have
\mathbf{g}_{b,c}+\mathbf{g}_{c,d}\approx_{a}\mathbf{g}_{b,d}
.

The term stems from the fact that these graphs satisfy (up to
\approx_{a}
) a
condition like Kirchhoff’s law on voltage drops: The weight sum of every cycle is zero.
In our case, adding up the
\mathbf{g}_{b,c}
along a cycle will yield zero up to
\approx_{a}
.
This implies the following property:

Lemma 7.5.

If
e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell},f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in R_{i}
satisfy
a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}e_{j}

and
a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}f_{j}
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
and
\pi
is a
permutation of
\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
, then in a Kirchhoff graph for
a
,
we have

\mathbf{g}_{e_{1},f_{1}}+\cdots+\mathbf{g}_{e_{\ell},f_{\ell}}\approx_{a}\mathbf{g}_{e_{1},f_{\pi(1)}}+\cdots+\mathbf{g}_{e_{\ell},f_{\pi(\ell)}}.

Proof.

Let us first observe that for any
e\in\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}\}
and
f\in\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\}
, the relation

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{e,f}+\mathbf{g}_{e^{\prime},f^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\approx_{a}(\mathbf{g}_{e,e^{\prime}}+\mathbf{g}_{e^{\prime},f})+(\mathbf{g}_{e^{\prime},e}+\mathbf{g}_{e,f^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\approx_{a}(\mathbf{g}_{e,e^{\prime}}+\mathbf{g}_{e^{\prime},e})+(\mathbf{g}_{e^{\prime},f}+\mathbf{g}_{e,f^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\approx_{a}\mathbf{g}_{e^{\prime},f}+\mathbf{g}_{e,f^{\prime}}

follows from the definition of Kirchhoff graphs. This is the case of

(3)

\mathbf{g}_{e_{1},f_{1}}+\cdots+\mathbf{g}_{e_{\ell},f_{\ell}}\approx_{a}\mathbf{g}_{e_{1},f_{\pi(1)}}+\cdots+\mathbf{g}_{e_{\ell},f_{\pi(\ell)}}

where
\pi
is a transposition, i.e. a permutation that swaps two points and lets all others fixed. Since every permutation of
\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
can be written as a composition of several transpositions,
Eq.

3
follows in full generality.
∎

Lemma 7.6.

If
G
is
i
-bidirected, then for each
a\in R_{i}
, there exists a Kirchhoff graph for
a
.

Proof.

Write
\{b\in R_{i}\mid a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b\}=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\}
. To simplify
notation, we write
\mathbf{g}_{r,s}
instead of
\mathbf{g}_{b_{r},b_{s}}
. We
have to pick
\mathbf{g}_{j,j}=\mathbf{0}
. Since
G
is
i
-bidirected, we
know that
\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}
is symmetric. In particular, for any

j\in[1,n-1]
, there exists a
\mathbf{g}_{j,j+1}
such that
b_{j}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,b_{j+1}+\mathbf{g}_{j,j+1}
. Moreover,
i
-bidirectedness of
G

guarantees that there exists a
\mathbf{g}_{j+1,j}
with

\mathbf{g}_{j+1,j}\approx_{b_{j}}{\mathbf{g}_{j,j+1}}^{\dagger}
such that

b_{j+1}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,b_{j}+\mathbf{g}_{j+1,j}
. Note that since
a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b_{j}

and
\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}
is symmetric, we have
\Delta_{b_{j}}=\Delta_{a}
and thus

\mathbf{g}_{j+1,j}\approx_{a}{\mathbf{g}_{j,j+1}}^{\dagger}
. Finally, for

r,s\in[1,n]
with
r<s
, we pick

\mathbf{g}_{r,s}=\mathbf{g}_{r,r+1}+\cdots+\mathbf{g}_{s-1,s}
and similarly

\mathbf{g}_{s,r}=\mathbf{g}_{s,s-1}+\cdots+\mathbf{g}_{r+1,r}
.

Let us now show that this is indeed a Kirchhoff graph for
a
. We
clearly have
b_{r}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,b_{s}+\mathbf{g}_{r,s}
for any
r,s\in[1,n]
. It
remains to show that

\mathbf{g}_{r,s}+\mathbf{g}_{s,t}\approx_{a}\mathbf{g}_{r,t}

for any
r,s,t\in[1,n]
. It suffices to do this
in the case that
|s-t|=1
, because the other cases follow by induction.
Consider the case
t=s+1
(the case
s=t+1
is analogous). We have to
show that

\mathbf{g}_{r,s}+\mathbf{g}_{s,s+1}\approx_{a}\mathbf{g}_{r,s+1}.

If

r\leq s
, then both sides are identical by definition. If
r>s
, then

\mathbf{g}_{r,s}
is defined as

\mathbf{g}_{r,s}=\mathbf{g}_{r,s+1}+\mathbf{g}_{s+1,s}
. By the choice of

\mathbf{g}_{s+1,s}
, we know that

\mathbf{g}_{s+1,s}\approx_{a}{\mathbf{g}_{s,s+1}}^{\dagger}
and thus

\mathbf{g}_{s+1,s}+\mathbf{g}_{s,s+1}\approx_{a}\mathbf{0}
. Hence:

\mathbf{g}_{r,s}+\mathbf{g}_{s,s+1}=\mathbf{g}_{r,s+1}+\mathbf{g}_{s+1,s}+\mathbf{g}_{s,s+1}\approx_{a}\mathbf{g}_{r,s+1}.\qed

For
Theorem

7.4
, we need two additional lemmas. The first follows
from property
(5)
and induction on
i
.

Lemma 7.7.

For bidirected
G=(N,T,P)
and
a\in R_{i}
, we have
D_{a}\subseteq H_{a}
.

Next observe that
L_{a}
contains vectors obtained by
adding, but also
subtracting
effects of derivation steps. We now show that if our
grammar is
i
-bidirected, then each such subtraction can be realized by a
sequence of ordinary derivation steps: The lemma says that every element of

H_{a}
can be written as a positive sum of derivation effects (up to a
difference in
D_{a}
).

Lemma 7.8.

If
G
is
i
-bidirected, then

H_{a}=\{-b+\mathbf{u}\mid\exists b\in R_{i}\colon b\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}~\text{and}~a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b\}^{*}+D_{a}

for every
a\in R_{i}

Here,
F^{*}
denotes the submonoid of
\mathbb{Z}^{N\cup T}
generated by the set
F
.
We are now ready to prove
Theorem

7.4
.

Proof of
Theorem

7.4
.

We begin with the inclusion
“
\subseteq
”. A simple induction on the length of a derivation shows
that every element of
M(a)
belongs to
M_{a}
.

Lemma

7.7
tells us that
D_{a}\subseteq H_{a}
, and since

D_{a}\subseteq S_{i}
, this implies
M_{a}+D_{a}\subseteq M_{a}
, hence

M(a)+D_{a}\subseteq M_{a}
.

We now prove “
\supseteq
”. An element of
M_{a}
is of the form
a+\mathbf{v}
with
\mathbf{v}\in H_{a}
and
a+\mathbf{v}\in S^{\prime}_{i}
. We claim that then
a+\mathbf{v}
belongs to
M(a)+D_{a}
.
Since
\mathbf{v}\in H_{a}
,
Lemma

7.8
tells us that

\mathbf{v}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}-b_{j}+\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j}

with
b_{j}\in R_{i}
,
a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b_{j}
,
\mathbf{u}_{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{R_{i}}
,
\mathbf{x}_{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{N_{[i+1,k]}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T_{[i,k]}}
where

b_{j}\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j}
for
j\in[1,n]
.

Since
G
is
i
-bidirected by
Lemma

7.3
,

Lemma

7.6
yields a Kirchhoff graph for
a

with weights
\mathbf{g}_{b,c}
for any
b,c\in R_{i}
with
a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b
and

a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}c
.
Let us now construct a derivation in
G
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
that
\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{\ell}\neq\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{u}_{\ell+1}=\cdots=\mathbf{u}_{n}=\mathbf{0}
.
For each
j\in[1,\ell]
, we pick some nonterminal
c_{j}\in R_{i}
such
that
c_{1}=a
and
\mathbf{u}_{j-1}(c_{j})>0
for
j\in[2,\ell]
. By our choice
of the
\mathbf{g}
’s, we can now derive as follows. We use the derivation
steps
b_{j}\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j}
. But since it is possible that

b_{j+1}\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}_{j+1}+\mathbf{x}_{j+1}
cannot be applied after

b_{j}\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j}
, we use derivations
c_{j}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,b_{j}+\mathbf{g}_{c_{j},b_{j}}
as connecting derivations. Here, we think of the

\mathbf{g}_{c_{j},b_{j}}
as “garbage” that we produce in order to use the
connecting derivations. Afterwards, we will
cancel out these garbage elements. We begin with a connecting derivation
in order to apply
b_{1}\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}_{1}+\mathbf{x}_{1}
:

\displaystyle a=c_{1}

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,b_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{c_{1},b_{1}}

\displaystyle\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}_{1}+\mathbf{x}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{c_{1},b_{1}}=a+(-c_{1}+\mathbf{u}_{1})+\mathbf{g}_{c_{1},b_{1}}.

Since
c_{2}
must occur in
a+(-c_{1}+\mathbf{u}_{1}+\mathbf{x}_{1})+\mathbf{g}_{c_{1},b_{1}}
, we can apply
c_{2}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,b_{2}+\mathbf{g}_{c_{2},b_{2}}
, etc. Doing this
\ell
times yields

a\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,a+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}-c_{j}+\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j}+\mathbf{g}_{c_{j},b_{j}}.

Let us denote the sum on the right-hand side by
\mathbf{y}
.
Since want to derive
a+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}(-b_{j}+\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j})
instead of
a+\mathbf{y}
, we now need to correct two aspects: (i) Our derivation subtracted
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}
instead of
b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}
, so we need to add
c
’s and subtract
b
’s and (ii) we need to cancel out the garbage elements
\mathbf{g}_{c_{j},b_{j}}
.
When replacing
b
’s by
c
’s, it could be that some
c
’s are equal to
b
’s, so
for (i), we don’t have to change those.
So we pick a permutation
\pi
of
\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
and a number
r\in[1,\ell]
so that (a)
c_{j}=b_{\pi(j)}
for
j\in[1,r]
and (b)
\{b_{\pi(r+1)},\ldots,b_{\pi(\ell)}\}
and
\{c_{r+1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\}
are disjoint. Now observe that the nonterminals
\{b_{\pi(r+1)},\ldots,b_{\pi(\ell)}\}
are never consumed in the derivation arriving at
a+\mathbf{y}
. However, since
a+\mathbf{v}\in S_{i}
, we know that
b_{1}+\cdots+b_{\ell}
must occur in
\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{u}_{j}
. Therefore, in particular
b_{\pi(r+1)}+\cdots+b_{\pi(\ell)}
must occur in
\mathbf{y}
.
But this means we can, for each
j\in[r+1,\ell]
, apply the derivation
b_{\pi(j)}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,c_{j}+\mathbf{g}_{b_{\pi(j)},c_{j}}
to
\mathbf{y}
(in any order). Thus, from
a
, using
\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,
, we can derive

\displaystyle a+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}-c_{j}+\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j}+\mathbf{g}_{c_{j},b_{j}}+\sum_{j=r+1}^{\ell}-b_{\pi(j)}+c_{j}+\mathbf{g}_{b_{\pi(j)},c_{j}}

\displaystyle=a+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}-b_{j}+\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j}+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{g}_{c_{j},b_{j}}+\sum_{j=r+1}^{\ell}\mathbf{g}_{b_{\pi(j)},c_{j}}\right).

Moreover, since
a+\mathbf{v}\in S_{i}
, we know that
b_{\ell+1}+\cdots+b_{n}
must occur in
\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}-b_{j}+\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j}
, and so we can just apply the steps
b_{j}\,\Rightarrow_{i}\,\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j}
for
j\in[\ell+1,n]
in any order to obtain:

a\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,a+\sum_{j=1}^{n}-b_{j}+\mathbf{u}_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{j}+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{g}_{c_{j},b_{j}}+\sum_{j=r+1}^{\ell}\mathbf{g}_{b_{\pi(j)},c_{j}}\right).

Now since
a+\mathbf{v}\in S^{\prime}_{i}
, the right-hand side contains no more level-
i
nonterminals. Hence, the right-hand side belongs to
M(a)
.
Finally, since
\pi(j)=j
for
j\in[1,r]
and
\mathbf{g}_{c_{j},c_{j}}\approx\mathbf{0}
for
j\in[1,\ell]
, the term in parentheses belongs to
D_{a}
by
Lemma

7.5
. Hence,
a+\mathbf{v}\in M(a)+D_{a}
.

Finally, note that
L(a)+D_{a}=L_{a}
follows from
M(a)+D_{a}=M_{a}
, because
D_{a}\subseteq S_{i}
and thus
L(a)+D_{a}=(M(a)\cap S_{i})+D_{a}=(M(a)+D_{a})\cap S_{i}=M_{a}\cap S_{i}=L_{a}
.
∎

While
Theorem

7.4
describes what nonterminals
a\in R_{i}
can derive,
we also need an analogue
a\in N_{i}\setminus R_{i}
.
It is a straightforward consequence of previous steps:

Corollary 7.9.

Suppose
G
is
i
-bidirected.
(1) For
a\in N_{i}
for
i\in[1,k]
,
we have
L(a)\neq\emptyset
iff there is some
a^{\prime}\in N_{i-1}

and a production
a\to a^{\prime}
such that
K_{a^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
.
(2) For
a\in N_{0}
, we have
L(a)\neq\emptyset
iff
there is some production
a\to\mathbf{u}
with

\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{N}^{N_{0}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T}
such that
K_{a\to\mathbf{u}}\neq\emptyset
.

Constructing coset circuits

We will
express our cosets in compact representations called
coset circuits
. Let
Y
be a finite
set. A
matrix representation
of a coset
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{Y}
is a matrix

\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}^{X\times Z}
and a vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{X}
such that
Y\subseteq Z
and

S=\{\pi_{Y}(\mathbf{x})\mid\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}\}
, where
\pi_{Y}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}^{Y}
is the
projection onto
\mathbb{Z}^{Y}
. Note that for a coset given as
S=\mathbf{v}+\langle\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{n}\rangle
, we can directly compute a matrix representation.
A
coset circuit over
\mathbb{Z}^{Y}
is a directed
acyclic graph
C
, whose vertices are called
gates
and

(1)

Leaves, i.e. gates
g
with in-degree
0
, are labeled by a matrix representation of a coset
C(g)
.

(2)

Gates with in-degree
>0
are labeled by
+
or
\cap
.

In a coset circuit, each gate
g
evaluates to a coset
C(g)
of
\mathbb{Z}^{Y}
(or the
empty set): The leaves evaluate to their labels. A gate
g
with
incoming edges from
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
,
m\geq 1
, evaluates to either

C(g_{1})+\cdots+C(g_{m})
or
C(g_{1})\cap\cdots\cap C(g_{m})
depending on whether

v
’s label is
+
or
\cap
.

We will construct a coset circuit that has for each
a\in N
, a gate for

H_{a},L_{a},M_{a},K_{a}
and also for
K_{a\to\mathbf{u}}
for productions
a\to\mathbf{u}\in P
.
By
Corollary

7.9
, this lets us check
emptiness of
L(a)
for each
a\in N
. For
H_{a},L_{a},K_{a\to\mathbf{u}}
with
a\in N_{0}
, we can directly create leaves with matrix representations. For the
others, the definitions do not tell us directly how to compute the cosets using
sums and intersections, e.g.:
H_{a}
is defined by a (potentially infinite)
generating set. Thus, we first show that
H_{a}
is generated by finitely many
cosets.

Lemma 7.10.

Let
G=(N,T,P)
be a bidirected
k
-grammar.
For every
a\in R_{i}
,
i\in[1,k]
, we have

H_{a}=\langle-b+L_{c}\mid b\in R_{i},c\in R_{i-1},a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b,b\to c\in P\rangle.

Proof.

By definition, we have
H_{a}=\langle-b+L(c)\mid b\in R_{i},c\in R_{i-1},a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b,b\to c\in P\rangle
. Since

Theorem

7.4
tells us that
L_{c}=L(c)+D_{c}
, the inclusion
“
\subseteq
” is immediate. For “
\supseteq
”, because of

L(c)+D_{c}
, we shall prove that
D_{c}\subseteq H_{a}
. (Note that this is
not an immediate consequence of
Lemma

7.7
, because
a\in R_{i}
and
c\in R_{i-1}
are on different levels).

For
D_{c}\subseteq H_{a}
, it suffices to prove that every
generator
e+{e}^{\dagger}
with
e\in R_{i}
,
c\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}e
, of
D_{c}
belongs
to
H_{a}
. Since
c\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}e
,
e
appears on a right-hand side of a
production and thus
L(e)\neq\emptyset
. Hence, there is some

\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N_{[i+1,k]}\cup T}
with
e\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,\mathbf{u}
. This implies that

e+{e}^{\dagger}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\Rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}_{i}\,\mathbf{u}+{\mathbf{u}}^{\dagger}
. Since
\mathbf{u}+{\mathbf{u}}^{\dagger}\in D_{a}\subseteq H_{a}
(
Lemma

7.7
) and

e+{e}^{\dagger}-(\mathbf{u}+{\mathbf{u}}^{\dagger})\in H_{a}
, this proves that
e+{e}^{\dagger}\in H_{a}
.
∎

This is a straightforward consequence of
Theorem

7.4
.
We have
now described each coset in terms of other cosets using sum, intersection, but
also
generated subgroup
(such as in
Lemma

7.10
). In order to
describe cosets only using sums and intersections, we use:

Lemma 7.11.

Let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and let
U,S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{m}
be subgroups with
(g_{i}+U)\cap S\neq\emptyset
for all
i\in[1,n]
. Then

(4)

\langle(g_{1}+U)\cap S,\ldots,(g_{n}+U)\cap S\rangle=(\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle+U)\cap S.

In other words,
Lemma

7.11
says that instead of imposing the
condition of belonging to
S

locally
at each summand
g_{i}+U
, it
suffices to impose it
globally
on the sum
\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle+U
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.11
.

The inclusion “
\subseteq
” is obvious because
U
and
S
are subgroups.
Conversely, suppose
h=x_{1}g_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}g_{n}+u\in S
for some
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
u\in U
.
Since
(g_{i}+U)\cap S\neq\emptyset
, we can choose
u_{i}\in U
for each
i\in[1,n]
such that
g_{i}+u_{i}\in S
.
We compute in the quotient
A/S
. Note that since
h\in S
and
g_{i}+u_{i}\in S
, we have

[u]=-[x_{1}g_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}g_{n}]=[x_{1}u_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}u_{n}]

and thus
u-x_{1}u_{1}-\cdots-x_{n}u_{n}\in S
.
Therefore, we have

(5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}h=\underbrace{x_{1}(g_{1}+u_{1})}_{\in\langle(g_{1}+U)\cap S\rangle}&~+~\cdots~+~\underbrace{x_{n}(g_{n}+u_{n})}_{\in\langle(g_{n}+U)\cap S\rangle}\\
&~+~\underbrace{u-x_{1}u_{1}-\cdots-x_{n}u_{n}}_{\in U\cap S}.\end{split}

This proves that
h
belongs to the left-hand side of
Eq.

4
, since that set is closed under adding
U\cap S
.
∎

Hence, if we have built gates for
U
and
S
and are given vectors

g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}
, then to create a gate for
\langle(g_{1}+U),\ldots,(g_{n}+U)\rangle\cap S
, we can create one for
(\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle+U)\cap S
. Since we can directly compute a matrix representation for
\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle
, we can
introduce a leaf for this subgroup.

Let us now describe how, using
Lemma

7.11
, each coset can be
expressed using only sums and intersection. To simplify notation, for any

a\in N_{i}
, we write
P_{a}=\{(b,c)\mid b\in N_{i},c\in N_{i-1},a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b,b\to c\in P\}
. Note that

\displaystyle H_{a}

\displaystyle=\langle-b+\mathbf{u}\mid a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b,~b\to\mathbf{u}\in P\rangle

\displaystyle\text{for $a\in R_{0}$},

\displaystyle H_{a}

\displaystyle=\langle-b+L_{c}\mid(b,c)\in P_{a}\rangle

\displaystyle\text{for $a\in R_{[1,k]}$}.

Thus, for
a\in R_{0}
, we have a finite generating set for
H_{a}
given explicitly in the grammar and can thus create a leaf gate for
H_{a}
labeled by an explicit matrix representation for
H_{a}
. However, for
a\in R_{[1,k]}
, we need to eliminate the
the
\langle\cdot\rangle
operator. To this end, we write

(6)

\begin{split}H_{a}&=\langle-b+L_{c}\mid(b,c)\in P_{a}\rangle\\
&=\sum_{(b,c)\in P_{a}}\langle(-b+c+H_{c})\cap S_{i-1}\rangle\\
&=\sum_{(b,c)\in P_{a}}(\langle-b+c\rangle+H_{c})\cap S_{i-1}\end{split}

where in the first step, we plug in the definition of
L_{c}
and in the second step, we apply
Lemma

7.11
. Here, the sum on the right only uses those
(b,c)\in P_{a}
for which
(\langle-b+c\rangle+H_{c})\cap S_{i-1}\neq\emptyset
. Now, each
\langle-b+c\rangle
is a group for which we can create a gate with an explicit representation.

We also need to express
K_{a}
in terms of sum and intersection. First note that for
b\in R_{i+1}
,

(7)

\begin{split}\langle-b+M_{b}\rangle&=\langle-b+((b+H_{b})\cap S^{\prime}_{i+1})\rangle\\
&=\langle(-b+S^{\prime}_{i+1})\cap H_{b}\rangle\\
&=H_{b}\cap\langle-b+S^{\prime}_{i+1}\rangle\end{split}

provided that
(-b+S^{\prime}_{i+1})\cap H_{b}\neq\emptyset
; otherwise,
\langle-b+M_{b}\rangle
is the trivial group
\{\mathbf{0}\}
.
In the first equality, we plug in the definition of
M_{b}
. The second is due to the definition of
S^{\prime}_{i+1}
, and the third applies
Lemma

7.11
, relying on
(-b+S^{\prime}_{i+1})\cap H_{b}
being non-empty. This implies that for
a\in N_{i}
,

(8)

\begin{split}K_{a}&=(L_{a}+\langle-b+M_{b}\mid b\in R_{i+1},a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b\rangle)\cap S_{i+1}\\
&=\left(L_{a}+\sum_{b\in R_{i+1},a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b}\langle-b+M_{b}\rangle\right)\cap S_{i+1}\\
&=\left(L_{a}+\sum_{b\in R_{i+1},a\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}b}H_{b}\cap\langle-b+S^{\prime}_{i+1}\rangle\right)\cap S_{i+1},\end{split}

where the last sum only uses those
H_{b}\cap\langle-b+S^{\prime}_{i+1}\rangle
for which
(-b+S^{\prime}_{i+1})\cap H_{b}\neq\emptyset
. The equality follows from the definition of
K_{a}
and
Eq.

7
. Note that for
-b+S^{\prime}_{i+1}
, it is again easy to construct a matrix representation.

Finally, observe that all these coset definitions rely on the relation
\leadsto
. On
N_{0}
, we can compute
\leadsto
directly. On
N_{[1,k]}
, we have to rely on cosets. To this, note that for
a,b\in N_{i}
, we have
a\leadsto b
if and only if there is a
c\in R_{i-1}
with a production
a\to c
and some
\mathbf{u}\in L_{c}
with
\mathbf{u}(b)=1
. In other words, if and only if
L_{c}\cap O_{b}\neq\emptyset
, where
O_{b}
is the coset
\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N\cup T}\mid\mathbf{u}(b)=1\}
.

Input :
Bidirected
k
-grammar
G=(N,T,P)

Create gates for
S_{i}
,
S^{\prime}_{i}
,
O_{a}
, and
\langle-a+b\rangle
for each
i\in[1,k]
,
a,b\in N

Compute
\leadsto
on
N_{0}

Create gates for
H_{a}
,
L_{a}
, and
M_{a}
for each
a\in R_{0}

Create gates for
K_{a\to\mathbf{u}}
for productions
a\to\mathbf{u}
,
a\in N_{0}
,
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{N}^{N_{0}}+\mathbb{Z}^{T}

for

i=1,\ldots,k

do

Create gate for
L_{c}\cap O_{b}
for each
c\in R_{i-1}
,
b\in N_{i}

Compute the relation
\leadsto
on
N_{i}
based on non-emptiness of
L_{c}\cap O_{b}
with
c\in N_{i-1}
,
b\in N_{i}

Compute
\mathrel{\mathop{\leadsto}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\vss}}}
on
N_{i}
and then
P_{a}
for each
a\in N_{i}

Create gate for
H_{a}
, then for
L_{a}
, then for
M_{a}
for each
a\in R_{i}
according to
Eqs.

6
,
2
and
8

Create gate for
K_{a}
for each
a\in R_{i-1}

Algorithm 1

Construction of coset circuit for a bidirected
k
-grammar

The exact order in which the gates are constructed is given in

Algorithm

1
. We observe that the constructed coset circuit
has depth
\leq ck
for some constant
c
: The gates for
H_{a}
,
L_{a}
,
M_{a}

only depend on gates created in the last iteration of the for-loop. Moreover,
each of them only adds a constant depth to the gates produced before. The
gates
K_{a}
depend on gates
H_{b}
created in the same iteration, thus also
adding only constant depth. Finally, note that the entries in the matrices in
the labels of the leaves require at most polynomially many bits: The gates for

S_{i}
,
S^{\prime}_{i}
,
O_{a}
,
\langle-a+b\rangle
and for
H_{a}
,
L_{a}
, and
M_{a}
for

a\in R_{0}
are obtained directly from the productions of
G
. The numbers in
those, in turn, have at most polynomially many bits as they are computed using

Theorem

6.5
.

From coset circuits to equations

In each of our three algorithms for
\mathsf{BIREACH}
, we check emptiness of coset
circuits by translating them into integer linear equation systems. Let

n=|Y|
. We compute, for each gate
g
, a matrix
\mathbf{A}_{g}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s_{g}\times t_{g}}

and a vector
\mathbf{b}_{g}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s_{g}}
such that
C(g)=\{\pi_{n}(\mathbf{x})\mid\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{t_{g}},~\mathbf{A}_{g}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}_{g}\}
, where for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, by
\pi_{n}
,
we denote the projection
\pi_{n}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\mathbb{Z}^{n}
onto the last
n

coordinates for any
r\geq n
. If
g
has in-degree
0
, then
g
is
already labeled with such a matrix
\mathbf{A}
and vector
\mathbf{b}
. Now suppose

g
has incoming gates
g_{1},\ldots,g_{r}
. Let
\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{A}_{r}
and

\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{r}
with
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s_{i}\times t_{i}}
,

\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s_{i}}
, denote the matrices and vectors constructed for the
gates
g_{1},\ldots,g_{r}
. Then the matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s\times t}
and

\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}
for
g
have the shape

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbf{A}_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\mathbf{A}_{r}\end{array}&\mathbf{0}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{C}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{b}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{b}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right)

where
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{C}
are chosen depending on whether
g
is labeled with
+

or
\cap
.
If the label is
+
, then
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times(t_{1}+\cdots+t_{r})},\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}
, are chosen so that
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{c}
expresses that in
\mathbf{x}
, the last
n
coordinates are the sum of
\mathbf{y}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{y}_{r}
, where for each
i\in[1,r]
, the vector
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
contains the coordinates

t_{1}+\cdots+t_{i}-n,\ldots,t_{1}+\cdots+t_{i}
, i.e. the last
n

coordinates corresponding to
\mathbf{A}_{i}
. Hence, we have
s=s_{1}+\cdots+s_{r}+n
and
t=t_{1}+\cdots+t_{r}+n
.
If the label is
\cap
, then
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}^{rn\times(t_{1}+\cdots+t_{r})},\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{Z}^{rn\times n}
are chosen so that
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{c}
expresses that for each

i\in[1,r]
, the last
n
coordinates of
\mathbf{x}
agree with
coordinates
t_{1}+\cdots+t_{i}-n,\ldots,t_{1}+\cdots+t_{i}
of
\mathbf{x}
, i.e. the last
n

coordinates corresponding to
\mathbf{A}_{i}
. Thus, we obtain
s=s_{1}+\cdots+s_{r}+rn
and
t=t_{1}+\cdots+t_{r}+n
.

Thus, in any case, we have
\max\{s,t\}\leq(r+1)\cdot\max_{i}\max\{s_{i},t_{i}\}
.
If
\mathbf{A}
is a matrix, then we define its
norm
, denoted
\|\mathbf{A}\|
, as the maximal
absolute value of any entry.
Observe that the norm in the above constructions does not grow at all: We have

\|\mathbf{A}\|\leq\max\{\|\mathbf{A}_{i}\|\mid i\in[1,r]\}
and
\|\mathbf{b}\|\leq\max\{\|\mathbf{b}_{i}\|\mid i\in[1,r]\}
. Suppose
m
is an upper bound on the number of rows and
columns of the matrices in the leafs of
C
, and
M
is an upper bound on
their norm. Then for any gate
g
of depth
i
, the resulting matrix

\mathbf{A}_{g}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s_{g}\times t_{g}}
and vector
\mathbf{b}_{g}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s_{g}}
satisfy

\max\{s_{g},t_{g}\}\leq(r+1)^{i}\cdot m
and
\|\mathbf{A}_{g}\|,\|\mathbf{b}_{g}\|\leq M
.
Hence, the dimensions of the matrices grow
exponentially in the depth of the circuit, but polynomially for fixed depth.
Moreover, their entries require no more bits than the matrices in the leaves.

Proposition 7.12.

(1) For every fixed number
d\in\mathbb{N}
, the problem
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\mathsf{SC}^{\pm}_{d})
is in
\mathsf{EXP}
.
(2) If
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
is fixed as well, then
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\mathsf{SC}^{\pm}_{d,\ell})
is in
\mathsf{P}
.
(3)
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\mathsf{SC}^{\pm})
is in
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
.

Proof sketch.

We begin with (1) and (2). According to
Proposition

7.1
, we need
to show that emptiness for bidirected
k
-grammars is in

\mathsf{EXP}
, and in
\mathsf{P}
for fixed
k
.

Given a bidirected
k
-grammar, we construct a coset circuit
C
as
described in
Algorithm

1
by alternating emptiness
checks for gates and building new gates. To check gates for
emptiness, recall that the circuit has depth
\leq ck
for some
constant
c\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, it remains to decide emptiness of a gate
g

in exponential time, resp. in polynomial time for bounded depth
circuits. For each gate
g
in this coset circuit, we compute a
matrix representation for the coset
C(g)
. As argued above, the
resulting matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s\times t}
and vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}
will
satisfy
s,t\leq(r+1)^{ck}
, where
r
is the largest in-degree of a
gate in
C
. The norm of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{b}
is at most the norm
of the matrices in the leaves of
C
, which means the entries of
\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{b}
only require polynomially many bits. Therefore, the size of

\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{b}
is polynomial in
(r+1)^{ck}
. Hence, by

Theorem

3.1
, we can decide emptiness of
C(g)
in time polynomial
in
(r+1)^{ck}
.
Hence,
Theorem

3.1
yields the desired algorithms.

For (3), we proceed slightly differently. Instead of

Proposition

7.1
, we use a variant with an exponential space
reduction. The resulting grammars still use polynomially many
productions, but the occurring numbers can be doubly exponential. Our
matrix encoding thus yields matrices with exponential dimension and
their entries have exponentially many bits. This still allows us to
check for emptiness of coset circuits in exponential time, resulting
in an
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
algorithm overall.
∎

Appendix A
Additional material for Section
4

A.1.
\mathsf{BIREACH}
and subgroup membership

See
4.2

Proof.

Consider a bidirected valence system
\mathcal{A}
over
\Gamma
with state set
Q
and two states
s,t\in Q
.
Let
(Q,E)
be the undirected graph where
E=\{\{p,q\}\mid p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\vss}}}q\}
.
First we compute a spanning forest
F\subseteq E
in logspace:
Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
be an enumeration of the edges in
E
.
Then we include an edge
e_{i}=(p,x,q)
in
F
if and only if there exists no path between
p
and
q

in the subgraph
(Q,\{e_{1},\dots,e_{i-1}\})
.
This can be tested in deterministic logspace
Reingold 2008
.
If
s
and
t
are disconnected in
F
, we can return any no-instance of
\mathsf{SUBMEM}(\Gamma)
.
Otherwise we restrict
\mathcal{A}
and
F
to the connected component of
s
.
Then we compute a set of transitions
T
which contains
for every edge
\{p,q\}\in F
a transition
p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\vss}}}q
and its reverse transition
q\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bar{x}$}\vss}}}p
.
Observe that the undirected version of
T
is a tree.
Between any two states
p,q
there is a unique simple path using only transitions from
T
.
Any other path from
p
to
q
which uses only transitions from
T
has the same effect
since it is obtained from the simple path from
p
to
q
by repeatedly inserting loops
r\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\vss}}}r^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bar{x}$}\vss}}}r
.
Let
w_{0}
be the label of the unique simple path from
s
to
t
using transitions from
T
.

Let
C=\{[w]\in\mathbb{M}\Gamma\mid s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}t\}
and
S=\{[w]\in\mathbb{M}\Gamma\mid t\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}t\}
.
Clearly
S
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{M}\Gamma
and
C
is a left coset of
S
in
\mathbb{M}\Gamma
,
namely
C=[w_{0}]S
. It remains to compute a generating set for
S
.
For any transition
\tau=(p,x,q)
of
\mathcal{A}
with
\tau\notin T
consider the unique cycle
\gamma_{\tau}\colon t\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle u$}\vss}}}p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\vss}}}q\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle v$}\vss}}}t

where the paths
t\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle u$}\vss}}}p
and
q\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle v$}\vss}}}t
are simple and only use transitions from
T
.
We claim that the effects of the cycles
\gamma_{\tau}
for all
\tau\in E\setminus T
generates
S
.
Consider any cycle
\gamma\colon q_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{1}$}\vss}}}q_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{2}$}\vss}}}\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{n}$}\vss}}}q_{n}
in
E
where
q_{0}=q_{n}=t
.
We show that
x_{1}\dots x_{n}
can be written as the product of effects of cycles
\gamma_{\tau}
and their inverses
by induction on the number of transitions in
\gamma
not contained in
T
.
If the cycle only uses transitions from
T
then we must have
x_{1}\dots x_{n}=1
.
Otherwise, suppose that
i
is minimal with
\tau=(q_{i-1},x_{i},q_{i})\notin T
.
Let
\gamma_{\tau}\colon t\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle u$}\vss}}}q_{i-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{i}$}\vss}}}q_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle v$}\vss}}}t
, which satisfies
[u]=[x_{1}\dots x_{i-1}]
.
We can replace
\gamma
by the cycle

t\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle u$}\vss}}}q_{i-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{i}$}\vss}}}q_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle v$}\vss}}}t\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bar{v}$}\vss}}}q_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{i+1}$}\vss}}}\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{n}$}\vss}}}q_{n}

without changing its effect.
Since
t\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bar{v}$}\vss}}}q_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{i+1}$}\vss}}}\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{n}$}\vss}}}q_{n}
uses strictly fewer transitions not contained in
T
than
\gamma

we can write
[\bar{v}x_{i+1}\dots x_{n}]
as a product of effects of cycles
\gamma_{\tau}
and their inverses.
This concludes the proof.
∎

See
4.1

Proof.

Let
\Gamma
be a looped graph.
Reducing
\mathsf{SUBMEM}(\Gamma)
to the problem
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\Gamma)
is easy:
To test
[w]\in\langle[w_{1}],\dots,[w_{k}]\rangle

we construct a bidirected valence system
\mathcal{A}
with two states
s,t

and the transitions
s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bar{w}$}\vss}}}t

and
t\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w_{i}$}\vss}}}t
for all
1\leq i\leq k
, and the reverse transitions.
Then
[w]\in\langle[w_{1}],\dots,[w_{k}]\rangle
holds if and only if there exists
u\in X_{\Gamma}^{*}

with
s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle u$}\vss}}}t
and
[u]=1
.

Conversely, we can compute in logspace a coset representation
\{[w]\in\mathbb{M}\Gamma\mid s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}t\}=[w_{0}]\langle[w_{1}],\dots,[w_{n}]\rangle

by
Lemma

4.2
.
Then it remains to test whether
[\bar{w}_{0}]\in\langle[w_{1}],\dots,[w_{n}]\rangle
.
∎

A.2.
Undecidability

In the following we will give the complete proof of
Theorem

3.4
.
The observation behind our proof is that using two non-adjacent vertices we can simulate a free group of rank two,
i.e.
\mathbb{M}\Gamma_{2}
where
\Gamma_{2}
consists of two non-adjacent looped vertices
a
and
b
.
The
\mathsf{P}
-hardness in
Theorem

3.2
also follows from this observation.
Suppose that
u,v
are non-adjacent vertices in
\Gamma
.
If both
u
and
v
are looped, then they already generate a free group of rank two.
Now suppose that
u
is unlooped.
We first simulate a pushdown over four letters using the words
W=\{uv^{i}\mid i\in[1,4]\}
,
i.e. for all
x\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}
we have
x\equiv_{\Gamma}\varepsilon
if and only if
x\leftrightarrow_{R}^{*}\varepsilon

where
R=\{(w_{i}\bar{w}_{i},\varepsilon)\mid i\in[1,k]\}
, see
Lemma

A.1
.
Then we can transform a bidirected valence system
\mathcal{A}
over
\Gamma_{2}

into a bidirected valence system
\mathcal{B}
over
\Gamma
.

If
\Gamma^{-}
contains
\mathsf{C4}
as an induced subgraph then we have two pairs
u_{1},v_{1}
and
u_{2},v_{2}
of non-adjacent vertices
where vertices from different pairs commute.
Applying the above reduction to every pair, we can reduce
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\mathsf{C4}^{\circ})
to
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\Gamma)
,
and obtain undecidability of
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\Gamma)
.

Lemma A.1.

Let
\Gamma
be a graph with two non-adjacent vertices
u
and
v
where
u
is unlooped.
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
W=\{w_{1},\dots,w_{k}\}\subseteq\{u,v\}^{*}

such that for all
x\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}

we have
x\equiv_{\Gamma}\varepsilon
if and only if
x\leftrightarrow_{R}^{*}\varepsilon

where
R=\{(w_{i}\bar{w}_{i},\varepsilon)\mid i\in[1,k]\}
.

Proof.

First we claim that, if
x\equiv_{\Gamma}\varepsilon
where
x\in X_{\Gamma}^{*}
then
x
does not contain any factor
of the form
uv^{i}\bar{u}
or
u\bar{v}^{i}\bar{u}
with
i\geq 1
.
This is true because such a factor in
x
cannot be eliminated by deletions of
u\bar{u}
,
v\bar{v}
or
\bar{v}v
.

Define
w_{i}=uv^{i}
and let
W=\{w_{i}\mid i\in[1,k]\}
.
We clearly have
\equiv_{R}\,\subseteq\,\equiv_{\Gamma}
.
For the converse we prove the following stronger statement:
For all
x\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}
with
x\equiv_{\Gamma}\varepsilon
we have

x\equiv_{R}\varepsilon
and any occurrence of
w_{i}\bar{w}_{j}
in
x
satisfies
i=j
,
We proceed by induction on
|x|
where the case
|x|=0
is clear.
If
|x|>0
then
x
must contain either
u\bar{u}
,
v\bar{v}
, or, if
v
is looped,
\bar{v}v
.
Since any occurrence of
u
in a word from
W
is followed by
v
,
and any occurrence of
\bar{v}
in a word from
W
is followed by
\bar{v}
or
\bar{u}
,
it must be the case that
v\bar{v}
occurs in
x
.
Again by the definition of
W
, the word
x
is of the form
x=suv^{m}\bar{v}^{n}\bar{u}t

where
m,n\in[1,k]
and
s,t\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}
.
We have
x\equiv_{\Gamma}suv^{m-1}\bar{v}^{n-1}\bar{u}t
.
By the remark from the beginning we must have
m=n=1
or
m,n\geq 2
.
If
m=n=1
then
x\equiv_{\Gamma}st
.
If
m,n\geq 2
then
x\equiv_{\Gamma}sw_{m-1}\bar{w}_{n-1}t\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}

and, by induction hypothesis, we obtain
m-1=n-1
, which also implies
x\equiv_{\Gamma}st
.
In both cases we obtain by the induction hypothesis that
x\equiv_{R}st\equiv_{R}\varepsilon
and that any occurrence of
w_{i}\bar{w}_{j}
in
st
satisfies
i=j
.
Hence also occurrence of
w_{i}\bar{w}_{j}
in
x=sw_{n}\bar{w}_{n}t
satisfies
i=j
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.2.

Let
\Gamma
be a graph with two non-adjacent vertices
u
and
v
.
There exists a finite set
W\subseteq\{u,v\}^{*}
and a morphism

\varphi\colon(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}\to X_{\Gamma}^{*}
such that
for all
y\in X_{\Gamma}^{*}
we have

y\equiv_{\Gamma^{\circ}}\varepsilon
if and only if there exists

x\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}
such that
x\equiv_{\Gamma}\varepsilon

and
\varphi(x)=y
.
Furthermore,
\varphi(w)\in X_{\Gamma}
for all
w\in W
and
\varphi(\bar{w})=\overline{\varphi(w)}
for all
w\in W

(it respects inverses).

Proof.

If
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\circ}
, i.e. both
u
and
v
are looped, then we set
W=V
and
\varphi
to be the identity.
Now assume that
u
is unlooped and let
W=\{w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4}\}
be a set from
Lemma

A.1
,
satisfying
x\equiv_{\Gamma}\varepsilon
if and only if
x\leftrightarrow_{R}^{*}\varepsilon

where
R=\{(w_{i}\bar{w}_{i},\varepsilon)\mid i\in[1,k]\}
.
Let
\varphi\colon(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}\to X_{\Gamma}^{*}
be the morphism defined by

\displaystyle w_{1}\mapsto u\qquad w_{2}\mapsto v\qquad w_{3}\mapsto\bar{u}\qquad w_{4}\mapsto\bar{v}

\displaystyle\bar{w}_{1}\mapsto\bar{u}\qquad\bar{w}_{2}\mapsto\bar{v}\qquad\bar{w}_{3}\mapsto u\qquad\bar{w}_{4}\mapsto v

We claim that for all
y\in X_{\Gamma}^{*}
we have

y\equiv_{\Gamma^{\circ}}\varepsilon
if and only if there exists

x\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}
such that
x\leftrightarrow_{R}^{*}\varepsilon

and
\varphi(x)=y
.
For the “if”-direction observe that any derivation
x\leftrightarrow_{R}x_{1}\leftrightarrow_{R}\dots\leftrightarrow_{R}x_{n}=\varepsilon

can be translated into a derivation

\varphi(x)\leftrightarrow_{\Gamma^{\circ}}\varphi(x_{1})\leftrightarrow_{\Gamma^{\circ}}\dots\leftrightarrow_{\Gamma^{\circ}}\varphi(x_{n})=\varphi(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon

since
\varphi(w_{i})\varphi(\bar{w}_{i})\equiv_{\Gamma}\varepsilon
.

For the “only if”-direction consider a derivation
y\to_{\Gamma^{\circ}}y_{1}\to_{\Gamma^{\circ}}\dots\to_{\Gamma^{\circ}}y_{n}=\varepsilon
.
We prove by induction over
n
that there exists
x\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}
with
x\to_{R}^{*}\varepsilon
and
\varphi(x)=y
.
By induction hypothesis there exists
x_{1}\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}
with
x_{1}\to_{R}^{*}\varepsilon
and
\varphi(x_{1})=y_{1}
.
There exist
s,t\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}
such that
x_{1}=st
,
y_{1}=\varphi(s)\varphi(t)

and
y_{1}=\varphi(s)\,z\,\varphi(t)
where
z\in\{u\bar{u},v\bar{v},\bar{u}u,\bar{v}v\}
.
We need to choose
x\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}
such that
x\to_{R}x_{1}
and
\varphi(x)=y
:

•

If
z=u\bar{u}
then set
x=sw_{1}\bar{w}_{1}t
.

•

If
z=v\bar{v}
then set
x=sw_{2}\bar{w}_{2}t
.

•

If
z=\bar{u}u
then set
x=sw_{3}\bar{w}_{3}t
.

•

If
z=\bar{v}v
then set
x=sw_{4}\bar{w}_{4}t
.

This concludes the proof.
∎

See
3.4

Proof.

Let us assume that
\Gamma^{-}\cong\mathsf{C4}
.
We want to show that
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\Gamma)
is undecidable
by a reduction from
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\mathsf{C4}^{\circ})
, which is undecidable by
Theorem

4.4
and
Theorem

4.1
.

Let the vertex set of
\Gamma^{-}
be
V=\{u_{1},v_{1},u_{2},v_{2}\}
with edges
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}
,
\{u_{1},v_{2}\}
,

\{v_{1},u_{2}\}
,
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
.
Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be the subgraphs of
\Gamma
induced by
V_{1}=\{u_{1},v_{1}\}
and
V_{2}=\{u_{2},v_{2}\}
, respectively.
By
Lemma

A.2
there exist finite sets
W_{1}\subseteq V_{1}^{*}
,
W_{2}\subseteq V_{2}^{*}
and morphisms

\varphi_{1}\colon(W_{1}\cup\bar{W}_{1})^{*}\to(V_{1}\cup\bar{V}_{1})^{*}
,

\varphi_{2}\colon(W_{2}\cup\bar{W}_{2})^{*}\to(V_{2}\cup\bar{V}_{2})^{*}
such that
for all
i\in\{1,2\}
and
y\in(V_{i}\cup\bar{V}_{i})^{*}
we have

y\equiv_{\Gamma_{i}^{\circ}}\varepsilon
if and only if there exists

x\in(W_{i}\cup\bar{W}_{i})^{*}
such that
x\equiv_{\Gamma_{i}}\varepsilon

and
\varphi_{i}(x)=y
.
Let
W=W_{1}\cup W_{2}
and let
\varphi\colon(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}\to(V\cup\bar{V})^{*}

be the unique morphism that extends
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
.
For all
y\in(V\cup\bar{V})^{*}
it satisfies
y\equiv_{\Gamma^{\circ}}\varepsilon

if and only if there exists
x\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}

with
x\equiv_{\Gamma}\varepsilon
and
\varphi(x)=y
.
Furthermore, the morphism satisfies
\varphi(\bar{w})=\overline{\varphi(w)}
for all
w\in W
by
Lemma

A.2
.

Given a valence system
\mathcal{A}
over
\mathsf{C4}^{\circ}\cong\Gamma^{\circ}
, and states
s,t

we want to test whether there exists a run
s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle y$}\vss}}}_{\mathcal{A}}t
with
y\equiv_{\Gamma^{\circ}}\varepsilon
.
We can assume that every transition in
\mathcal{A}
is labeled by a single symbol from
V\cup\bar{V}
, by splitting transitions.
Construct the valence system
\mathcal{B}
over
\Gamma
obtained by replacing every transition
p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle v$}\vss}}}q

by transitions
p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}q
for all
w\in W
with
\varphi(w)=v
.
Observe that
\mathcal{B}
is bidirected because the morphism respects inverses.
Moreover, there exists a run
s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\vss}}}_{\mathcal{B}}t
with
x\equiv_{\Gamma}\varepsilon
if and only if
there is a run
s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle y$}\vss}}}_{\mathcal{A}}t
with
y\equiv_{\Gamma^{\circ}}\varepsilon
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Additional material for Section
5

Lemma B.1.

\mathsf{BIREACH}(\Gamma)
is
\mathsf{L}
-hard under
\mathsf{AC}^{0}
many-one reductions for every
\Gamma
.

Proof.

We need to reduce from the reachability on undirected graphs to
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\Gamma)

since the former problem is
\mathsf{L}
-complete under
\mathsf{AC}^{0}
many-one reductions
Àlvarez & Greenlaw 2000
;
Reingold 2008
.
To do so, we simply replace every undirected edge between nodes
p
and
q

by transitions
p\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q
and
q\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}p
.
∎

Lemma B.2.

If
\mathcal{G}
is closed under subgraphs and LC-unbounded then
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\mathcal{G})
is
\mathsf{ILD}
-hard under logspace many-one reductions

Proof.

Given a system of linear Diophantine equations
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
has columns
\mathbf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
.
Since
\mathcal{G}
is LC-unbounded and closed under induced subgraphs it contains a looped clique
\Gamma_{m}
with
m
nodes
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
.
Let
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}^{m}\to X_{\Gamma_{m}}^{*}
be the function mapping
(k_{1},\dots,k_{m})

to
v_{1}^{k_{1}}\dots v_{m}^{k_{m}}
.
Then we construct the valence system
\mathcal{A}
over
\Gamma_{m}
with two states
p
and
q

and the transitions
p\xrightarrow{\varphi(\mathbf{a}_{i})}p
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
p\xrightarrow{\overline{\varphi(\mathbf{b})}}q
,
and their reverse transitions.
Then
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
has a solution
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
if and only if there exists
w\in\Gamma_{m}^{*}
with
[w]=1
and
p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}q
.
∎

Lemma B.3.

If
\Gamma
has two non-adjacent vertices then
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\Gamma)
is
\mathsf{P}
-hard.

Proof.

We can assume that
\Gamma
only consists of two non-adjacent vertices
u
and
v
.
Observe that
\mathbb{M}\Gamma^{\circ}
is a free group over
u
and
v
.
Subgroup membership in the free group over two generators is
\mathsf{P}
-hard by
Avenhaus & Madlener 1984
,
and hence also
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\Gamma^{\circ})
by
Theorem

4.1
.
We can reduce from
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\Gamma^{\circ})
to
\mathsf{BIREACH}(\Gamma)
using
Lemma

A.2
,
similarly to the proof
Theorem

3.4
:
There exists a finite set
W\subseteq\{u,v\}^{*}
and a morphism
\varphi\colon(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}\to X_{\Gamma}^{*}

such that for all
y\in X_{\Gamma}^{*}
we have
y\equiv_{\Gamma^{\circ}}
if and only if
there exists
x\in(W\cup\bar{W})^{*}
with
x\equiv_{\Gamma}\varepsilon
and
\varphi(x)=y
.
Hence reachability in a bidirected valence system over
\Gamma^{\circ}
can be logspace reduced to
reachability in a bidirected valence system over
\Gamma
by replacing each transition
p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle v$}\vss}}}q
,
v\in X_{\Gamma}

by transitions
p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}q
for all
w\in W
with
\varphi(w)=v
.
∎

Lemma B.4.

If
\mathcal{G}
is closed under induced subgraphs and UC-unbounded then

\mathsf{BIREACH}(\mathcal{G})
is
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-hard under logspace many-one reductions.

Proof.

We reduce from the word problem over commutative semigroups, known to be
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-hard
Mayr & Meyer 1982
.
Since
\mathcal{G}
is UC-unbounded and closed under induced subgraphs, it contains an unlooped clique
\Gamma
of size
|\Sigma|
.
We can assume that
\Sigma
is its node set.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be the bidirected valence system over
\Gamma
with three states
q_{0},q_{1},q_{2}
,
the transitions
q_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bar{u}$}\vss}}}q_{1}
,
q_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle v$}\vss}}}q_{2}
,
and the transitions
q\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bar{x}y$}\vss}}}q
for all
(x,y)\in R
,
and their reverse transitions.
Then
u\equiv_{R}v
holds if and only if
q_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}q_{2}
for some
w\in X_{\Gamma}^{*}
with
[w]=1
.
∎

Appendix C
Additional material for Section
6

We need a few known results on commutative semigroups.
In the following we denote by
\Psi\colon\Sigma^{*}\to\mathbb{N}^{\Sigma}
the Parikh function
where
\Psi(w)(x)
is the number of occurrences of
x
in
w
.

Lemma C.1.

Let
(\Sigma\mid R)
be a commutative semigroup presentation.
If
u\equiv_{R}v
then there exists a derivation
u=u_{0}\to_{R}u_{1}\to_{R}\dots\to_{R}u_{n}=v

such that

|u_{i}|\leq\max\{|u|,|v|\}+\|R\|^{2^{|\Sigma|}}+\|R\|

for all
i\in[0,n]

where
\|R\|=|R|\cdot\max_{(x,y)\in R}|xy|
.
In particular, we can test whether
u\equiv_{R}v
and, if so, compute such a witnessing derivation in deterministic space
2^{O(|\Sigma|)}\log(\|R\|+|u|+|v|)
.

Proof.

The bound on the lengths
|u_{i}|
follows from Proposition and Lemma 3 in
Mayr & Meyer 1982
.
To compute the path we use Reingold’s logspace algorithm for undirected connectivity,
which also computes a path between two given vertices
Reingold 2008
.
Let
G
be the graph obtained from restricting
(\Sigma^{*},\to_{R})
to words
of length
\leq\max\{|u|,|v|\}+\|R\|^{2^{|\Sigma|}}+\|R\|

and identifying words having the same Parikh image.
Then
u\equiv_{R}v
if and only if they are connected by a path in
G
.
∎

The following statement from
Koppenhagen & Mayr 1997
, Lemma 17

describes the congruence classes of a commutative semigroup presentation as
hybrid linear sets
.

Lemma C.2.

Let
(\Sigma\mid R)
be a commutative semigroup presentation.
Then for any
u\in\Sigma^{*}

there exist base vectors
\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{b}_{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{\Sigma}

and period vectors
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}^{\Sigma}
such that

\Psi([u]_{\equiv_{R}})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\{\mathbf{b}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\lambda_{j}\mathbf{p}_{j}\mid\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}\}

and
\|\mathbf{b}_{1}\|,\dots,\|\mathbf{b}_{\ell}\|\leq(|u|+\|R\|)\cdot 2^{O(|\Sigma|)}
.

C.1.
\mathbb{N}
-counters

Fix a clique
\Gamma=(V,I)
where
U
and
L
are the sets of unlooped and looped vertices in
\Gamma
, respectively.
Furthermore, we are given a bidirected valence system
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\to)
over
\Gamma
,
and two states
s,t\in Q
.
For
Y\subseteq V
let
\pi_{Y}\colon X_{\Gamma}^{*}\to(Y\cup\bar{Y})^{*}
be the projection to the alphabet
Y\cup\bar{Y}
.

To transfer the results from
Lemmas

C.1
and
C.2
to the valence system
\mathcal{A}
,
we translate
\mathcal{A}
into an equivalent commutative semigroup presentation.
To this end, we use the standard method of eliminating the states of
\mathcal{A}
by introducing two additional counters.
Without loss of generality let the state set of
\mathcal{A}
be
Q=\{1,\dots,k\}
.
Furthermore, we can ensure that each transition
p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}q
is of the form
w=\overline{w^{-}}w^{+}

for some
w^{-},w^{+}\in U^{*}
, by splitting transitions and adding intermediate states.
Let
U^{\prime}=U\cup\{\alpha,\beta\}
where
\alpha,\beta
are fresh symbols.
Define the commutative semigroup presentation
(U^{\prime},R)

where
R
contains all pairs

(\alpha^{p}\beta^{k-p}\pi_{U}(w^{-}),\alpha^{q}\beta^{k-q}\pi_{U}(w^{+}))

for all transitions
p\xrightarrow{\overline{w^{-}}w^{+}}q
,
and all pairs
(xy,yx)
for
x,y\in U^{\prime}
.
Any path
p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}q
with
\Psi(w)=\mathbf{0}

can be translated into a derivation in
R
from
\alpha^{p}\beta^{k-p}
to
\alpha^{q}\beta^{k-q}
,
and vice versa.

See
6.1

Proof.

By
Lemma

C.1
one can determine in deterministic space
2^{O(|\Sigma|)}\log(\|R\|+|u|+|v|)

if there is a derivation in
R
from
\alpha^{p}\beta^{k-p}
to
\alpha^{q}\beta^{k-q}
and, if so, compute such a derivation,
which can then be translated into a path
s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}t
in
\mathcal{A}
with
\Psi(w)=\mathbf{0}
.
∎

See
6.2

Proof.

By
Lemma

C.2

there exist vectors
\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\mathbf{b}_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{U^{\prime}}

and vectors
\mathbf{p}_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{\ell}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{U^{\prime}}
such that

\Psi([\alpha^{s}\beta^{k-s}]_{\equiv_{R}})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\{\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\lambda_{j}\mathbf{p}_{j}^{\prime}\mid\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}\}

and
\|\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\prime}\|,\dots,\|\mathbf{b}_{\ell}^{\prime}\|\leq\|\mathcal{A}\|\cdot 2^{O(|U|)}
.
By setting
J_{q}=\{i\in[1,m]\mid\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\prime}(x)=q,\,\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\prime}(y)=k-q\}
we obtain

\mathsf{Reach}(s,q)=\bigcup_{i\in J_{q}}\{\mathbf{b}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\lambda_{j}\mathbf{p}_{j}\mid\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}\}

where
\mathbf{b}_{i}
and
\mathbf{p}_{j}
are the restrictions of
\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{p}_{j}^{\prime}
to
U
, respectively.

We set
B=\{u\in U\mid\mathbf{p}_{j}(u)=0\text{ for all }j\in[1,\ell]\}
and
b=\|\mathcal{A}\|\cdot 2^{O(|U|)}
,
which satisfy the properties claimed by the lemma.
Note that since we only use the existence of
\mathbf{b}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{m}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{p}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{\ell}^{\prime}
(and the complexity of computing them is not clear), it remains to show how to compute
B
.
Observe that
u\notin B
if and only if there exists
\mathbf{v}\in\mathsf{Reach}(s,s)

with
\mathbf{v}(u)>0
.
This can be decided as follows:
Let
\mathcal{A}_{u}
be obtained from
\mathcal{A}
by adding
a bidirected transition
s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bar{u}$}\vss}}}\bot
to a new state
\bot

with bidirected loops
\bot\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle v$}\vss}}}\bot
for
v\in U
.
Then there exists
\mathbf{v}\in\mathsf{Reach}(s,s)
with
\mathbf{v}(u)>0
if and only if
there exists
w\in X_{\Gamma}^{*}
with
s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}\bot
in
\mathcal{A}_{u}
and
\Psi(w)=\mathbf{0}
.
The latter can be decided in deterministic space
2^{O(|U|)}\cdot\log\|\mathcal{A}\|
by
Lemma

6.1
.
∎

C.2.
Adding
\mathbb{Z}
-counters

In the following we use the functions
\Phi_{Y}\colon X_{\Gamma}^{*}\to\mathbb{Z}^{Y}

defined by
\Phi_{Y}(w)=\Phi(w)|_{Y}
for
Y\subseteq V
,
and similarly
\Psi_{Y}\colon X_{\Gamma}^{*}\to\mathbb{B}^{Y}
.

See
6.3

Proof.

Let
B\subseteq U
be the set and the number
b=\|\mathcal{A}\|\cdot 2^{O(|U|)}
from
Lemma

6.2
.
The idea is to maintain the
B
-counters in the state
and the
(U\setminus B)
-counters using
\mathbb{Z}
-counters.
Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the looped clique with vertex set
L^{\prime}=(U\setminus B)\cup L
.
Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the valence system over
\Gamma^{\prime}
with the state set
Q\times[0,b]^{B}
.
In the following, we will often use the projection
\pi_{L^{\prime}}\colon(V\cup\bar{V})^{*}\to(L^{\prime}\cup\bar{L^{\prime}})^{*}
; to ease notation, we abbreviate it as
\pi
.
For every transition
p\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}q
in
\mathcal{A}
and vectors
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in[0,b]^{B}

we add a transition

(p,\mathbf{a})\xrightarrow{\pi(w)}(q,\mathbf{b})

to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
if
\mathbf{a}\oplus\Psi_{B}(w)=\mathbf{b}
. Clearly
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is bidirected.
In the remainder of the proof, we will show that

(9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{Eff}(s,s)=\{\Phi_{L}(w^{\prime})\mid(s,\mathbf{0})\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(s,\mathbf{0}),~\Phi_{U\setminus B}(w^{\prime})=\mathbf{0}\}.\end{split}

Once this is established, we are essentially done: This is because
Lemma

4.2
allows us to compute vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L^{\prime}}

such that
\{\Phi_{L^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})\mid(s,\mathbf{0})\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w^{\prime}$}\vss}}}(s,\mathbf{0})\}=\langle\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\rangle
.
With these, we have

\mathsf{Eff}(s,s)=\{\mathbf{v}|_{L}\mid\mathbf{v}\in\langle\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\rangle,\,\mathbf{v}|_{L^{\prime}\setminus L}=\mathbf{0}\},

which is the desired representation of
\mathsf{Eff}(s,s)
.

It remains to prove (
9
).
If

s=s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}s_{1}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow{w_{n}}s_{n}=s

is a run in
\mathcal{A}
with
\Psi_{U}(w_{1}\dots w_{n})=\mathbf{0}

then

(10)

(s_{0},\mathbf{b}_{0})\xrightarrow{\pi(w_{1})}(s_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1})\xrightarrow{\pi(w_{2})}\dots\xrightarrow{\pi(w_{n})}(s_{n},\mathbf{b}_{n})

is a run in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

where
\mathbf{b}_{i}=\Psi_{B}(w_{1}\dots w_{i})
for all
i\in[0,n]
and
\mathbf{b}_{n}=\mathbf{0}
.
Observe that
\Psi_{U}(w_{1}\dots w_{i})\in\mathsf{Reach}(s,s_{i})
and hence
\mathbf{b}_{i}\in[0,b]^{B}
by
Lemma

6.2
.
Furthermore the run (
10
) satisfies

\displaystyle\Phi_{L}(\pi(w_{1})\dots\pi(w_{n}))

\displaystyle=\Phi_{L}(w_{1}\dots w_{n}),

\displaystyle\Phi_{U\setminus B}(\pi(w_{1})\dots\pi(w_{n}))

\displaystyle=\Phi_{U\setminus B}(w_{1}\dots w_{n})=\mathbf{0}.

Conversely, suppose there is a run

(s_{0},\mathbf{b}_{0})\xrightarrow{\pi(w_{1})}(s_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1})\xrightarrow{\pi(w_{2})}\dots\xrightarrow{\pi(w_{n})}(s_{n},\mathbf{b}_{n})

in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

with
s_{0}=s
and
s_{n}=s

for some run
s_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w_{1}$}\vss}}}\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w_{n}$}\vss}}}s_{n}
satisfying
\mathbf{b}_{i}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{i}\Psi_{B}(w_{j})=\Psi_{B}(w_{1}\dots w_{i})
and
\mathbf{b}_{n}=\mathbf{0}
.
Furthermore we have
\Phi_{U\setminus B}(w_{1}\dots w_{n})=\Phi_{U\setminus B}(\pi(w_{1})\dots\pi(w_{n}))=\mathbf{0}
.
Let
\mathbf{u}_{i}=\Psi_{U\setminus B}(w_{1}\dots w_{i})
for all
i\in[0,n]
.
Let
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
c\oplus\mathbf{u}_{i}(u)\in\mathbb{N}
for all
i\in[0,n]
and
u\in U\setminus B
.
By assumption there exists a run
s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}s
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\Psi_{U}(w)\in\mathbb{N}^{U}

and
\Psi_{U}(w)(u)\geq c
for all
u\in U\setminus B
.
Then

s\xrightarrow{w}s\xrightarrow{w_{1}\dots w_{n}}s\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}s

is a run in
\mathcal{A}

with

\Phi_{L}(ww_{1}\dots w_{n}\bar{w})=\Phi_{L}(w_{1}\dots w_{n})=\Phi_{L}(\pi(w_{1})\dots\pi(w_{n})).

To prove
\Psi_{U}(ww_{1}\dots w_{n}\bar{w})=\mathbf{0}
we consider
B
and
U\setminus B
separately.
We have

\displaystyle\Psi_{B}(ww_{1}\dots w_{n}\bar{w})

\displaystyle=\Psi_{B}(w)\oplus\Psi_{B}(w_{1}\dots w_{n})\oplus\Psi_{B}(\bar{w})

\displaystyle=\Psi_{B}(w)\oplus\Psi_{B}(\bar{w})=\mathbf{0}.

where the second equality follows from
\mathbf{b}_{n}=\mathbf{0}
and the third equality follows from
\Psi_{B}(w)\in\mathbb{N}^{B}
.
To show
\Psi_{U\setminus B}(ww_{1}\dots w_{n}\bar{w})=\mathbf{0}
,
we only need to prove that
\Psi_{U\setminus B}(ww_{1}\dots w_{i})\in\mathbb{N}^{U\setminus B}
for all
i\in[0,n]

since we know that
\Phi_{U\setminus B}(w_{1}\dots w_{n})=\mathbf{0}
.
This holds because

\displaystyle\Psi_{U\setminus B}(ww_{1}\dots w_{i})(u)=\,

\displaystyle\Psi_{U\setminus B}(w)(u)\,\oplus

\displaystyle\Psi_{U\setminus B}(w_{1}\dots w_{i})(u),

\Psi_{U\setminus B}(w)(u)\geq c
and
c\oplus\Psi_{U\setminus B}(w_{1}\dots w_{i})(u)\in\mathbb{N}
for all
u\in U\setminus B
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

See
6.4

Proof.

Given a clique
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}
and a bidirected valence system
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\to)
over
\Gamma
,
and states
s,t\in Q
.
Using
Lemma

6.1
we can test whether there is a path
s\mathrel{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\vss}}}t
with
\Psi(w)=\mathbf{0}
,
and, if so, we find
\mathbf{u}:=\Phi(w)\in\mathsf{Eff}(s,t)
.
This works in exponential (logarithmic) space if
\mathcal{G}
consists of cliques of unbounded (bounded) size.
By
Proposition

6.3

we can compute a representation
\mathsf{Eff}(s,s)=\{\mathbf{v}|_{L}\mid\mathbf{v}\in\langle\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\rangle,\,\mathbf{v}|_{L^{\prime}\setminus L}=\mathbf{0}\}
.
for some
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L^{\prime}}
where
L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and
|L^{\prime}|\leq|\Gamma|
.
We need to test whether
\mathbf{0}\in\mathsf{Eff}(s,t)
, which is equivalent to
-\mathbf{u}\in\mathsf{Eff}(s,s)
.
This holds if and only if there exists
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}(u)=-\mathbf{u}(u)
for all
u\in L

and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}(u)=0
for all
u\in L^{\prime}\setminus L
,
which is a system of
|\Gamma|
equations.
This can be solved in
\mathsf{ILD}
.
If
|\Gamma|
is bounded then this is in
\mathsf{L}
(even
\mathsf{TC}^{0}
)
Elberfeld et al. 2012
, Theorem 13
.
∎

For the proof of
Theorem

6.5
, we need a slightly stronger
version of
Theorem

3.1
. Specifically, the algorithm in

Chou & Collins 1982
is not only able to decide whether an
equation
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
,
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
,
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, has an integral solution
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. It can even, in
polynomial time, compute a set
\{\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k}\}
of integral vectors
such that
\langle\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k}\rangle
is exactly the set of
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with

\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
.
Such a set
\{\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k}\}
is called a
general solution
of the equation
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
.

Theorem C.3 (
Chou & Collins 1982
).

Given a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
, one can compute in polynomial
time a general solution for the equation
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
.

See
Chou & Collins 1982
, Theorems 1 and 13
.

See
6.5

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of
Proposition

6.4
.
To compute a generating set for
\mathsf{Eff}(s,s)
we compute the vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L^{\prime}}

with
\mathsf{Eff}(s,s)=\{\mathbf{v}|_{L}\mid\mathbf{v}\in\langle\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\rangle,\,\mathbf{v}|_{L^{\prime}\setminus L}=\mathbf{0}\}
,
which is a projection of the solution set of a linear Diophantine system.
Therefore, by
Theorem

C.3
, we can compute in polynomial time
\mathbf{u}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{L}
with

\mathsf{Eff}(s,s)=\langle\mathbf{u}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{m}\rangle
.
Using
Lemma

6.1
we can test whether
\mathsf{Eff}(s,t)\neq\emptyset
, and,
if so, find
\mathbf{u}\in\mathsf{Eff}(s,t)
in exponential space (log-space if
\mathcal{G}
is UC-bounded).
This gives us the representation
\mathbf{u}+\langle\mathbf{u}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n}\rangle
for
\mathsf{Eff}(s,t)
.
∎

Appendix D
Additional material for Section
7

D.1.
The reduction to grammar emptiness

In this section, we prove
Proposition

7.1
.

The idea behind all these grammar translations is to simulate the runs of
valence systems over the disjoint union of graphs: Such a run is always obtained
by starting from a run with neutral effect over one component, then inserting a run with neutral effect over a
different component, then again inserting a run from some component, etc.
Here, a key trick is to use a nonterminal symbol
a_{p,q}
for each pair of
states that represents a run from
p
to
q
. Then, inserting a run from
p
to

q
for
a_{p,q}
yields a new run. For general reachability, this works even if
we introduce nonterminals
a_{p,q}
for which there does not exist a run:
Such an
a_{p,q}
will never be replaced and causes no issues.

However, if we want to guarantee that our grammar is bidirected (we will define
this later precisely), we always have to make sure that every derivation can be
reverted. In particular, every nonterminal that can be produced should be able
to derive something. This property will be captured in our notion of
“realizable placeholder runs” which we define next.

Decomposition into tree

First, we want to make the tree structure in the input graphs
\Gamma\in\mathsf{SC}^{\pm}_{d}
explicit.
We may assume that our input graph has an unlooped vertex that is adjacent to all other vertices: Otherwise, we can just add such a vertex.
Our graph
\Gamma=(V,I)
in
\mathsf{SC}^{\pm}_{d}
has a tree structure. We decompose its vertices into a tree
t
accordingly:

(1)

Consider the set
U\subseteq V
of vertices in
\Gamma
that are adjacent to all other vertices.

(2)

If
U
is a strict subset of
V
, then
\Gamma\setminus U
has at least two connected components.
Then we construct the tree for each connected component. The tree for
\Gamma
is obtained by taking these trees and adding a parent node containing
U
.

(3)

Otherwise,

\Gamma
is a clique with
\leq d
unlooped vertices. Then
t
contains one node with all of
\Gamma
.

Given a valence system
\mathcal{A}
over
\Gamma
, a
placeholder
is a triple

(p,{s}^{\tiny{\hbox to3.65pt{\vbox to3.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.825pt\lower-1.825pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

or

. The set of placeholders is denoted by

as a subtree or if (ii)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

.

Given a subtree
s
of
t
, the automaton

\mathcal{A}_{s}
is obtained from
\mathcal{A}
by deleting all edges labeled with

v\in\Gamma
that are incomparable to
s
.
For each
s^{x}\in\{{s}^{\tiny{\hbox to3.65pt{\vbox to3.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.825pt\lower-1.825pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}},{s}^{\tiny{\hbox to3.65pt{\vbox to3.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.825pt\lower-1.825pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.14 L -2.35 -2.35 L 2.35 -2.35 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
